Part IV Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning for Security Solutions

Chapter 10 Video Image Identification and Verification Using Preliminary Score Approach for Authentication and Security Enhancement

ADITI UPADHYAY,8

Abstract

Image is one of the identities of a person, which reflects their emotions, feelings, age, etc. It helps gather information about a person without knowing their name, caste, or age. This may not be of much importance when used for domestic or framing applications. However, when images are involved in multimedia, privacy becomes a major concern for everyone. Everyone wants to receive the original image without it being tampered with by unauthorized individuals. Image authentication has gained much attention due to its wide usage in multimedia and various applications. The non-secure transmission of digital images over channels is increasing globally day by day through computer networks, making the authentication of each image a challenging task. Therefore, defense, space, medical, and quality measurement images must be secured against wrongful manipulation or changes to the original image. To ensure the authenticity of multimedia pictures, various methods have been proposed, including digital signatures, watermarking, cryptography, and fragility based on image contents. The objective of this chapter is to identify and verify real-time video images using a preliminary score approach. This approach helps to assess the authenticity of an image at an initial stage by extracting features, evaluating them using the Viterbi algorithm, and converting the input image into an embedded state. The achieved matrix is then transformed, and based on this, a preliminary score estimation is generated after multiple iterations for each image. Finally, the tested image is verified using the aforementioned approaches to protect and provide security to the original image. This approach can be useful in various surveillance applications for real-time image identification and verification.

10.1 Introduction

In the past few years, face identification and its verification have received great attention in the fields of computer vision, pattern recognition, and biometrics. Researchers are particularly interested in the remarkable ability to identify individuals, considering the relevance of human activity in both daily life and multimedia. Facial analysis involves extracting or gathering information such as age, gender, pose, landmarks, and identity. It has various applications, including law enforcement, face biometrics for transactions, self-driving vehicles, and active verification on devices [1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6].

Face identification and verification systems typically consist of three stages. The first stage requires a face identifier to locate faces in an image, which should possess strong properties to handle variations in illumination, scale, and pose. Additionally, an efficient face identifier should consistently produce accurate results while confining the area of interest [7]. The second stage localizes facial indicators such as the tip of the nose, eye centers, tip of the ear lobes, and corner of the mouth. These indicators are then used to align faces, taking into account scaling and in-plane rotation effects. The third stage involves a feature extractor that encodes identity statistics into a high-dimensional locator. These comprehensive descriptions are then used to evaluate similarity or score between similar faces. A robust feature extractor is capable of handling errors introduced by previous stages in the pipeline: face alignment, face detection, and landmark localization [8]. The steps involved in image identification and verification are shown in Figure 10.1. The face has many advantages that make it an efficient biometric for distinguishing individuals. First, the face reflects not only the identity but also the internal emotions (feelings) of happiness or sadness, including the approximate age of the person. Second, even at a long distance, capturing the face biometric is easy. This feature makes face recognition important in person recognition as well as in human-computer interaction [9].

The face biometric is affected by a number of extrinsic and intrinsic variations such as lighting, pose, age, and expressions. Over the past decades, there has been a significant improvement in the performance of face recognition, but it still remains below acceptable levels in many applications [10, 11, 12, 13, 14, 15 and 16]. Recent efforts have mainly focused on different features, 3D (three-dimensional) models, and video inputs to overcome the performance limitations of 2D still face recognition. Geometrically, the face is essentially a 3D space composed of a large number of polygons that can be represented by facial features and pixels related to the face geometry. Image data can be computationally represented in vector form. For example, an xxy 2D image can be mapped to a vector PeRxY by sequencing the pixel elements through concatenating each row and column of the image. Different faces with different emotions are shown in Figure 10.2. Where; P=(P1,P2...Pi...PN), denotes the nxN data matrix; where; P. is a face vector of dimension n, concatenated from xxy face image, where n=xx y denotes the total number of pixels.

10.2 Preliminary Score Approach

Face identification at the preliminary stage will be done using the hidden Markov model (HMM) with unobserved states. These states do not directly reflect the identity of the face but generate tokens in the form of sequences that highlight the information of the sequence state. Variable a(t)= { a i,a2,a3) is the state (hidden) with time ‘t’ and variable b(t)= {b1, b2,b3) at time ‘t.’ Now, using gray scale for 1D HMM, the facial area covers the hairs, forehead, eyes, nose, and mouth in an up-to-down order. The face recognition system is shown in Figure 10.3. It is observed that each facial area is assigned to a state from left to right in 1D. However, embedded HMMs in 2D have been utilized for image representation and recognition, Y={ Y,}={ Yo,} using spatial indices (p,q) and designed by HMM arrangement which consist of a set called super state Spq which does not allow any type of crossing between embedded state and super state [17, 18, 19, 20, 21, 22 and 23]. A fully comprehensive state includes five characteristics that properly cover the forehead, eyes, nose, mouth, and chin in a vertical direction, along with initial state and state transition probabilities. {Πv, Bv}={πfn;bfnFn+1}. Now, every state (super) itself is a 1D standard HMM containing an embedded state for facial modeling features and has 21 embedded states in the horizontal direction.

(1)
$$\begin{array}{l} {b_{pqr}(x) = p\left( {xIr_{pqr},p_{b}} \right)} \\ {= {\overset{'}{O}}_{1_{\text{pq}}}^{\text{N}_{1}}\text{w}_{\text{pq}}\text{rl}_{\text{pq}}\text{N}\left( {\text{xI}{\grave{\text{i}}}_{\text{pqr}}1_{\text{pq}},{\overset{'}{O}}_{\text{pqr}}1_{\text{pq}}} \right)} \\ \end{array}$$

The distribution of two different videos to attain values of different score levels is shown in Figure 10.4(a) and (b).

10.3 Image Authentication

Authentication means verifying the real value or content, which is achieved without being tampered with. In real-time applications, it is very difficult to maintain the original identity due to various reasons generated by the surroundings or communication devices. Image authentication does not accept any tampering in the image data. The conventional approach based on cryptography evaluates message authentication from images using a hash function. This hash function is then encrypted with the sender’s private key and attached to the image. For more secure data exchange between parties, the hash can be encrypted with the help of the beneficiary’s public key. The authentication and verification process are depicted in

Figure 10.5(a) and (b). The recipient evaluates the hash from the received image, which was attached to the recovered image and decrypted with the help of the private key. Hash evaluation of images using column and line functions is obtained, and these hashes are stored and compared with those obtained from each column and line of the image to be verified. If any mismatch in the hash function is observed, the image is declared to be tampered with; otherwise, it is announced as authentic. Since hash functions are very sensitive to any slight changes in binary data or image pixels, this puts limits on image verification for many applications. The fragile watermarking approach is used to create a watermark and place it in the image to be protected in such a way that any changes found are also reflected in the placed watermark. Verifying the existence of the placed watermark approves the authenticity of the image and finally localizes the damaged areas. This method does not accept any distorted image and authenticates only if all its pixels remain constant. The generation and verification process are depicted in Figure 10.6(a) and (b).

Fragile watermarking algorithms are based on forming a watermark from the identity of an image, and the watermark is evaluated from a set of image pixels. The set of pixels, taken from the secret key SKI, can be encrypted with another set’s least significant bits using the secret key SKI To enhance the security of the algorithm, the set of pixels can be determined by another secret key, SK2.

The purpose of a digital signature is to electronically sign a document, such as when signing a contract or withdrawing money from a bank. Digital signatures can be transferred electronically along with a signed letter. However, there are several issues associated with digital signatures.

Firstly, a digital signature requires an algorithm to be attached to the signature for electronic transfer. Secondly, there are problems related to verification and signature authentication. For example, when a person’s signature is verified by comparing it with their certified signature while making a purchase using a credit/debit card. This authentication approach is not strong enough, as it is easy to copy the signature of a certified person. Therefore, electronic signatures can be tested by anyone with knowledge of the verification algorithm.

Applying the digital signature algorithm directly to an image may result in situations where the image content has not been tampered with, but the algorithm declares the image as non-authentic. As a result, changes to existing digital signature methods must be made by defining the information that needs to be signed, such as signing the image content rather than the image data itself. Digitally signing the hash value using existing digital signatures like a public key or private key can increase overall security. The verification procedure depends on the image signature, which can be extracted from the attached signature and compared with the original signature [24, 25, 26, 27, 28, 29 and 30].

A hash function operates on message ‘X’ of any length to achieve a fixed-size hash ‘h’ called a message digest.

(2)
h = H(X)

where; H indicates hash function and size of h is usually smaller than ‘X’

The digital signature algorithm and hash function have the same features, which are used to compare the signature based on dissimilarity and selected characteristics. A variable length code can also be assigned to the digital signature for verification. Coefficient quantization is applied to obtain a {0,1} sequence, which can be encoded to form the digital signature. This algorithm is valid with 2 and 3 decomposition levels, but there is a trade-off between complexity, security, and processing time. Cryptography provides higher-level solutions for digital image security. Conventional cryptography provides strict authentication rules that determine the authenticity of an image based on its binary representation or changes in image pixels. This approach has good performance, but the localization of the evaluated areas is not up to the mark. The detection of tampered images was done well using the conventional cryptography algorithm approach. The subset of frames represented in Figure 10.7 under different poses, illuminations, and may be useful for identification purposes.

Video face verification requires matching all the frames in two videos. However, not all frames are equally informative and may have extreme variations in pose, image quality, illumination, and expression. Therefore, some frames may be damaged due to the presence of covariates, making it crucial to select and utilize video information efficiently and carefully.

10.4 Algorithm For Frame Selection And Average Score

The algorithm for video image frame selection and its estimated coefficients for maximum average score are discussed in the following steps:

  1. Video frame selection is based on the average using the discrete wavelet transform method, which first identifies the given input image (I) that can be evaluated as:


    II(DWT) = [IIap,Iv, h, dI]

    where; Iap represents the estimated coefficients of the image; and Iv,h,d contains coefficient details of vertical, horizontal, and verticals sidebands.

    Fusion of Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning for Advanced Image Processing, Data Analysis, and Cyber Security

  2. A filtering process will be applied using high pass and low pass filters for the decomposition of the parent wavelets.

  3. The next level of DWT has now been applied to the first approximation achieved in the first step approximation band.


    II(DWT) = [IIap,Iv, h, dI]

    where; IIap, Iv, h, dI denotes second level estimation of input image (I).

  4. The average of every DWT band is evaluated by dividing the image into xxy windows and capturing them.$H(j) = - \underset{i = 1}{\overset{m}{\sum^{}}}P\left( j_{i} \right)\log_{2}P\left( j_{i} \right)$ where; P(jj) is the probability mass function that indicates pixel values in probability form reflecting in the neighborhood; and m is the total number of pixels.
  5. If window size is L xW then: x y$P\left( j_{i} \right) = \frac{m_{ji}}{L_{x} \times W_{y}}$ where; mii denotes number of pixels in windows.
  6. The average of each window is now combined to evaluate the feature value of the band.$FH = \underset{i = 1}{\overset{\mu}{\sum^{}}}\left( \left| E_{i} \right| \right)$ where; FH indicates the feature score value; p is the number of windows; and Ei is the entropy of the ith window.
  7. Final total score of images I,FH (I) was obtained by averaging the feature value of each band individually:FH(I) = FH(IapI) + FH(Ih, v, dI) + FH(Ih, v, d)
  8. For a video image Vi; feature score of frames (fi) is denoted by FH(fi) and obtained max-min normalization using:$m_{min\operatorname{}} = \frac{FH\left( f_{i} \right) - \min(FH)}{\max(FH) - \min(FH)}$where; FH denotes all feature scores for Vi ; and min(FH) , max (FH) denotes the denotes the minimum and maximum value of FH.
  9. Once the score of each individual frame is evaluated, an adaptive process for frame selection is performed to identify the best set of frames.$\theta_{i} = \left\{ \begin{matrix} {1,\operatorname{}if\operatorname{}n_{i} \geq \sigma_{n} + \frac{\mu_{n}}{2}} \\ {0,otherwise\operatorname{}} \\ \end{matrix} \right.$ where; µpn denotes the mean appropriate for the feature value ; and σn represents the standard deviation.

  10. Testing was performed on the rich feature frame retrieved from the database and verified using the matched score for perfect authentication. The false acceptance and false rejection rates are higher for the feature trained image compared to Eigen, random, and normal feature images. From a security standpoint, it is necessary to keep these rates low in order to achieve a desirable high verification performance. However, the high false acceptance rate of the feature trained image is a positive indication of achieving a high verification rate. This is beneficial in maintaining security measures in real-time image applications such as video surveillance and authentication, as depicted in Figures 10.8 and 10.9.

10.5 Proposed Model For Identification And Verification

The proposed model for face identification and verification is shown in Figure 10.10. It is used to identify the succession of faces from the given input and track the identified face over successive frames. The model selects the most suitable and best face for proper identification, assuming that the background is previously known and fixed. If no face is detected, the system estimates the background by using information from the previous frame. In the final stage of the verification process, if a new face is identified, it is switched to the training stage to create a new face model. If the face is identified from the stored face in the database, the analogous face model is re-trained. Based on the proposed model, identification and verification have been performed on four input images (feature trained, Eigen, random, and random). The verification percentage is calculated by comparing the false acceptance rate of the observed results [31, 32, 33 and 34]. Real-time images have been captured from various sources such as YouTube videos, social networking sites, and our own database. The results have been simulated to achieve good performance, as discussed in the “Results and Discussions” section.

10.6 Results And Discussions

The experiments are performed with the images gathered from social networking videos, including your database, which contains 10-100 frames with a duration of 2-6 seconds. These frames have informativebased features that were first trained and tested using the proposed model mentioned above for verification percentages. We analyze the performance for a fixed number of frames, without considering adaptive methods or any frame selection algorithms.

The performance report depicted in Figures 10.11-10.14 indicate that the accuracy of feature-trained images for different subjects is more efficient compared to Eigen, random, and normal feature images. Instagram shows higher accuracy compared to other platforms such as Facebook, YouTube, and the self-database images. This may be due to the fact that Instagram images are unaltered and contain all their captured features, whereas YouTube images have lower accuracy compared to other images. Preliminary scores can help evaluate the overall score of each frame, and by averaging the scores at the maximum level, the desired verification rate can be achieved. Security levels can be further improved by assigning each image an unpredictable coding with a private key instead of hash coding (Tables 10.1-10.4).

Chapter 11 Machine Learning and Cyber Defense: Hype Vs. Reality

RENU, MEENA SACHDEVA, and INDERPREET KAUR

Department of CSE, GCET, Greater Noida, Uttar Pradesh, India

Abstract

Modern businesses need to secure crucial assets, ranging from critical infrastructures to personal information, from cyber-attacks while achieving optimized business processes. Artificial intelligence (AI) is being used with huge potential to support information security. Machine learning (ML) can be utilized to prevent our information from a number of attacks, but this is not an acceptable solution. The technology also helps to combine all customer data, which serves as input to produce new vulnerabilities. Different opinions consider it as a game between attackers and security providers. In this chapter, we discuss the application areas, true potential, and limitations of ML. ML-based technologies are improving business processes by providing opportunities to design defense mechanisms against large-scale frauds. However, attackers can also misuse it to launch new threats. We demonstrate the ways of utilizing ML by both defenders and attackers. An extensive review and experiments performed on real enterprise systems are taken into account to draw conclusions.

11.1 Introduction

Machine learning (ML) works as an important tool in enhancing the protection measures in attack detection systems. ML algorithms are grouped into supervised and unsupervised learning algorithms based on the input. Supervised learning relies on labeled training data, while unsupervised learning relies on unlabeled training data to infer the classes on its own [1]. A Comparative Survey covers the Influence of ML Techniques on cybersecurity. Various researchers and organizations have claimed that AI is not immune to attacks [2]. The chapter is divided into several sections. Section 11.2 illustrates the paradigms of ML algorithms for cyber defense. Section 11.3 outlines the application of ML algorithms for cybercrime. Section 11.4 includes the Survey and Evaluation, and Section 11.5 covers the current trends of ML algorithms in the cyber industry. Finally, Section 11.6 concludes the chapter with user awareness.

11.2 Paradigms of Machine Learning (ML) and Applications in Security

ML algorithms are commonly referred to as shallow learning (SL) and deep learning (DL). Both categories are utilized in cybersecurity, as shown in Figure 11.1.

Shallow techniques require a human expert who can identify the relevant data and its characteristics before the final implementation of the algorithm. On the other hand, DL works with multi-layered input data and automatically selects features through unlabeled learning [3]. Both SL and DL encompass numerous techniques. Popular techniques for SL algorithms include K-nearest neighbor (KNN), support vector machines (SVMs), naïve bayes (NB), logistic regression (LR), random forest (RF), hidden Markov models (HMMs), and shallow neural network (SNN) [4]. These techniques are mostly used for classification and are sometimes combined by a human expert. Deep neural networks (DNNs) serve as the foundation for any DL algorithms and consist of large neural networks capable of autonomous learning. Neurons in one layer may be fully connected, forming fully-connected feedforward deep neural networks (FNN), or partially connected to only a subset of the next layer, forming convolutional feedforward deep neural networks (CNN).

11.3 Machine Learning (Ml) For Cyber Crime

Criminals are also taking advantage of this tool in crafting malicious activities and targeting thousands of people every day. Performing such activity manually is a tedious and time-consuming job; hackers can automate this with the help of ML. These activities are categorized into 5 groups based on their target and level of harm:

  1. Information Gathering: This is the start of every cyberattack. More information means a higher probability of success in a cyberattack. Collected information can be organized and gathered both online and offline. Neural networks and their associated clustering and classification methods can be used to analyze victims’ posts on social networks. Modern networks are software-defined networks (SDN), where ML can assist adversaries. Hackers can gather information about security tools and other quality of service (QoS) related policies. An attacker can benefit from analyzing traffic and flow rules. Recently, the Social Mapper tool was designed to locate individuals across various social platforms using Google images.
  2. Impersonation: It allows attackers to target victims through various methods such as spamming, phishing, and spoofing. One of the oldest and most popular forms is email spam, where ML has been employed by cyber attackers. By training a neural network, the process of spamming can be automated, generating junk emails without raising suspicion. Impersonation through email is relatively simple, and a person’s behavior can be easily tracked through their posts. Recently, the Markov model has been utilized in conjunction with recurrent neural networks (RNNs) to generate new tweets based on previous ones.
  3. Unauthorized Access: To gain unauthorized access is common in two popular areas where ML can be of assistance. Captcha bypassing is the most popular method in mass hacking, where object segmentation is achieved through the support vector machines (SVMs) method. ML is utilized to break reCAPTCHA images with an accuracy of 82% [7]. Another area is password brute force, where passwords can be guessed with the help of ML. Markov models were first trained to generate a pattern with a set of similar passwords.
  4. ML for Attack: Most of the attacks are performed due to phishing, which leads to the download of malware, spyware, ransomware, or other malicious programs. Fuzzing is a common practice among hackers to discover system vulnerabilities. With the help of AI-based smarter techniques, new examples of documents can be rapidly found to crash any application. Additionally, vulnerability discovery can be accelerated in a cost-effective manner by using ML-based algorithms.
  5. Automate the Crime: Criminals can automate tasks involved in various activities. While there is no specific prediction about what will be automated, organizations related to cybercrime are hiring experts to develop software for their support.

11.4 Literature Survey And Evaluation

This section will cover the benefits and side effects of ML. It will also provide guidelines for industries to decide whether to apply ML algorithms. Currently, no algorithm can be applied without human intervention. There are two aspects that make ML a good choice for the industry. First, a large volume of useful data can be easily collected to provide an initial level of security. Various tools are designed to help sort, slice, and mine large volumes of data automatically, assisting security analysts. A lot of work has been done on intrusion detection systems (IDS) using ML. Understanding the data source is the first step for any security policy. Various methods have been tried to apply ML to IDS by different researchers. A very popular way of attack detection is Packet-Based, where packets provide all the communication-related details. Packet information is parsed first with the help of unsupervised learning. A fuzzy C-means based algorithm was introduced by Hu et al. [8]. Yang Tian-Yi et al. [9] proposed packet parsing methods and payload analysis methods. Aaron Adler et al. proposed Behavior-Based Access Control with attack Detection on TCP Connections and HTTP Requests. Gong et al. [10] provided a Feasibility Analysis of Data Transmission in SDN. Another problem is the availability of qualified, experienced security analysts. Designing efficient defensive policies is a complex game, where a single wrong decision is enough to open the door for vulnerabilities. AI-driven attacks are becoming very frequent, creating a demand for security experts in the current world, which is full of data everywhere. In the cyber world, organizations are still hesitant to trust ML-based defensive mechanisms. ML has been widely used by both hackers and security communities [11]. Naturally, trust with your peers is preferred compared to trust with a ML model. In the upcoming years, a combination of ML models and security experts will be preferred.

11.5 Current Trends Of Machine Learning (Ml) Algorithms In Cyber Security

Currently, a large majority of various ML approaches are being utilized to design “warning” systems, but they still require human experts for the final decision. Intrusion detection, malware analysis, and spam detection are three major areas where ML algorithms are applied [12]. ML models are being used as a defense under different names, some of which are popular and listed here:

  1. Splunk: It is a software that has a range of applications, from simple IT operations to analytics and cybersecurity. ML is used in User Behavior Analytics to identify digital weak points of users and respond to malware attacks quickly and automatically.
  2. Chronicle: It is a software that has a range of applications, from simple IT operations to analytics and cybersecurity. ML is used in User Behavior Analytics to identify digital weak points of users and respond to malware attacks quickly and automatically.
  3. Microsoft: Windows defender, named “advanced threat protection” (ATP), is exclusively designed for prevention, breach detection, and automated recovery. It utilizes cloud AI and ML algorithms at multiple levels to identify and mitigate threats.

11.6 Conclusion

Cyber attackers may target us anywhere, in any form. Important products or tools can be hijacked for the sake of money, or criminals can access valuable data that may go unnoticed for months. Machine learning (ML) is helping in the cyber world by providing security solutions and solving industry-specific problems, but not every problem. On the opposite side, criminal activities are also benefiting from ML to perform cybercrime faster and cheaper. A critical challenge in cybersecurity is to design adaptable and automated security policies to target new threats and vulnerabilities more effectively. It is very important to decide when and which algorithms can provide mature enough solutions. This chapter may help future researchers analyze existing solutions and the various issues of ML in sensitive environments. A strong security plan can be implemented in multiple layers of protection throughout computers, programs, and networks.

Chapter 12 An Effectual Investigation on Security and Privacy in Vehicular Ad-Hoc Networks with Preventive Measures

SHALLY NAGPAL, SHIVANI GABA, ALANKRITA AGGARWAL, and DEEPAK KUMAR

Panipat Institute of Engineering and Technology, Panipat, Samalkha, Haryana, India

Abstract

Vehicular Ad-hoc Networks (VANETs) can make streets more secure, cleaner, and more brilliant. It can offer a wide extend of administrations, which can be security and non-safety related. Numerous safety-related VANETs applications are real-time and mission basic, which would require strict ensure of security and unwavering quality. Indeed non-safety related interactive media applications, which is able play an critical part within the future, will require security back. Need of such security and protection in VANETs is one of the key preventions to the wide spread usage of it. An unreliable and untrustworthy VANET can be more dangerous than the framework without VANET bolster. So it is fundamental to create beyond any doubt that “life-critical safety” data is secure sufficient to rely on. Securing the VANETs together with suitable assurance of the security drivers or vehicle proprietors may be a exceptionally challenging errand. In this chapter we summarize the assaults, comparing security necessities and challenges in VANETs.

12.1 Introduction

Vehicular ad hoc networks (VANETs) receive significant attention from academics and the automobile industry. VANETs present an opportunity to enhance road safety by facilitating communication between vehicles, without the need for additional fixed infrastructure. VANET applications can be categorized into three subcategories: safety applications, non-safety applications, and basic applications. Safety applications focus on protecting users and informing them about road incidents such as traffic collisions. Non-safety applications aim to improve user convenience and contribute to traffic optimization. Furthermore, VANET provides real-time road safety information to assist drivers and road users in staying safe. However, the VANET ecosystem is not immune to threats, as it is susceptible to vulnerabilities inherent in vehicular networks.

Dedicated short-range communications (DSRC) operating in the 16.9 GHz frequency band is allowed with the wireless access in vehicular environment (WAVE) protocol, which is built on the IEEE 802.11p standard. Most vehicle-to-RSU connectivity is achieved using wireless networking, also known as vehicular wireless access or WAVE-based devices. WAVE connectivity ensures that travelers’ vehicles have up-to-date relevant information and accurately tracks the movement of traffic, improving pedestrian and rider safety. Additionally, it enhances traffic flow and the performance of the traffic management system. In the VANET, an OBU, an RSU, and a TA are units responsible for collecting valuable vehicle details such as speed, acceleration, power, and more [1]. The network then transfers this information to local vehicles through a wireless connection. RSU units are also connected to the TA through a wired network. The TA serves as the chief of all elements and is responsible for maintaining the VANET’s proper functioning.

Figure 12.1 depicts a classical scenario of a wireless-based vehicular network in which various vehicles are interconnected by wireless technologies [2].

  1. Remote Specific User (RSUs): Computer machines fixed along-side the highway or in designated locations such as parking lots or intersections. Cables are commonly used to help vehicles traveling on nearby roads stay connected. The RSUs include network equip-ment using IEEE 802.11p radio technologies for dedicated short-range networking (DSRC). Furthermore, RSUs are a particularly effective method of communication with other computers in other networks [3].
  2. Onboard Unit (OB): A GPS-based tracking system is almost always installed in any vehicle, allowing RSUs and other OBUs to monitor shared vehicle details. The RCP, sensors, interface, and read/write storage are all part of the electronics package that make up the OBU. The OBU has the primary responsibility to connect to another OBU through an IEEE 802.11p wireless connection. Additionally, the car battery serves as the primary power source for the OBU, and each vehicle contains sensors to monitor location and environmental conditions, an event data recorder (EDR), and motion sensors, all of which help provide feedback to the OBU [4].
  3. Trusted Authority (TA): It is responsible for the entire VANET scheme, including registering the network resources, providing operational guidelines, and overseeing vehicle use. It is also required to verify vehicle authentication, user ID, and OBU ID to ensure VANET protection and prevent any vehicle damage. In the event of any malicious messages or unusual activity, the TA will expose OBU ID and information [5]. The TA system is also capable of identifying the criminals.

12.2 Performance, Privacy, and Security Issues in Vanet

Because of the diverse implementations and facilities, such as improved traffic quality, passenger safety, and infotainment, VANETs have been extensively studied. Vehicle-to-everything (V2X) technologies must therefore address numerous technological challenges as they are less flexible, less capable, and provide low bandwidth and limited computing power. In VANETs, cloud computing is considered a potential solution to meet these requirements. However, conventional cloud computing may not meet the next-generation VANET standards for autonomous vehicles, which require high mobility, low latency, real-time implementations, and networking [6].

Most VANET implementations are either safety or non-safety oriented. In VANETs, safety applications are used to provide safety-related signals, such as warnings that help road vehicles respond to avoid dangerous conditions and incidents. Safety information includes road traffic updates, traffic jams, maintenance work, and emergency vehicle warnings. Low latency and high reliability are essential for these safety applications. On the other hand, non-safety uses of VANETs are very convenient and effective. Traffic control applications can be divided into two categories: protection and entertainment. In traffic management, software is used to optimize traffic flow and reduce congestion on the route. Informational and entertaining technologies are used to provide internet connectivity for travelers, such as storing records, downloading videos, and making phone calls. However, disseminating vital incident records, such as accident reports, accurately and rapidly is crucial in VANETs. Although VANETs can be used to disseminate event information, it is challenging to deliver timely and accurate information to a specific location while vehicles are in motion. This is due to the relatively small transmission range of DSRC and the contention-based carrier-sense multiple access scheme in IEEE 802.11p. MAC contention causes delays in vital messages, which is not ideal for VANETs. Failure to disseminate time-sensitive information in a timely and precise manner may result in collateral damage to nearby vehicles. The feasibility of cellular networks to support VANET services while providing large coverage and high data rates to vehicular users has recently been examined [7] (Figure 12.2).

In contrast, while a single wireless communications technology, such as DSRC or a cellular network, can support some ITS services in an area with heavy loads, high mobility, and a complex network topology, a complete ITS support from a single wireless communications technology, such as DSRC or a cellular network, might not be possible. A heterogeneous vehicular ad hoc network can be a potential medium that can satisfy many challenging connectivity criteria by combining cellular networks and IEEE 802.11p. Seamless data communication can be established between vehicles that are spatially separated in VANETs [1].

Traditionally, VANET technology has not been adequate for processing smart vehicle data including video and sensor data, which can produce enormous volumes of traffic data. Additional servers are needed in distributed areas in order to capture and process a vast volume of instantaneous traffic information. The cloud computing approach, coupled with VANETs, could be the best option for this kind of use.

Recent studies show that mobile users are now making use of cloud computing for complex computations that were difficult or impossible to perform locally. There have been a number of studies conducted on VANETs that incorporate cloud computing in order to advance the potential of VANETs even further. Most of the vehicles’ uploads and downloads use time and resources [8]. Currently, cloud storage cannot meet the location recognition, accessibility assistance, and latency standards for vehicle density in urban areas.

12.3 Challenges in Vanet-Based Environment

Due to vulnerabilities in VANET that cause information communication vulnerabilities, VANET is susceptible to attacks. In order to ensure vehicle-to-vehicle (V2V) and network stability, VANET security systems must meet specific requirements. While all networks must adhere to the same standards, only certain networks are required to do so for VANET. The conditions are as follows:

  1. Authentication: VANET should require vehicles to authenticate each other in order to enable V2V contact. This method can involve identifying the sender’s identity and verifying the authenticity of the sender.
  2. Availability: In this context, availability refers to the VANET system’s ability to be functional and operational at all times. Like any system, response time is an important consideration for any program.
  3. Privacy: It is critical in VANET. The name and location of the vehicle must be kept secret.
  4. Integrity: It refers to trust. In a free flow of content, there can be no modification attacks. Knowledge is reliable because of this.

A powerful, clean, insightful, and entertaining mass transit system relies on VANETs, which are also one of the foundational technologies in the field. Today, the time people spend in cars is huge. Smart vehicles based on VANETs have emerged to make the time more stable, reliable, friendly, pollution-free, and economical. More protection can be created by ensuring that important incidents, such as those related to safety, are reported accurately. Efficient transport is made possible by reducing road congestion and noise, and by improving travel time predictability. When you take VANETs on the go, they will connect to the Internet and provide you with downloadable information, as well as links to social networks. The signals VANETs use are either beacons or protection messages. Vehicles use transmitters to transmit and relay status information at 100-millisecond intervals. Beacons are sent to nearby vehicles and include the velocity, position, and pseudo-ID of the sender. Another perspective is that safety alerts often serve as emergency information for drivers on the road, helping them avoid potentially life-threatening conditions and enabling timely rescue if needed. Vessels broadcast a WAVE short message (WSM) as they encounter events on the route. The message payload includes vehicle position, message sending time, vehicle location, and local road activities. In vehicular contact range, each vehicle collects information about the other vehicles. We consider heterogeneous VANETs, where vehicles use IEEE802.11p and LTE cellular technology, to be heterogeneous as well [9].

Virtual LANs (VLANs) use remote specific user (RSUs) as gateways to connect to cloud providers on the go. Traffic and multimedia data are acquired by vehicular nodes through cloud services. Vehicles are located on the outer edge of the network, while the cloud is at the center. With a cloud-based VANET, it is possible to track the location of all cars in the network and estimate traffic patterns in real-time, enabling personalized safety alerts for those in need. While VANETs leverage the internet to address current challenges, the expansion of smart vehicles still requires addressing certain fundamental issues. Future VANETs are defined as a result of the rapid growth and improvement of VANETs [10].

12.4 Assaults and Threat Perspectives

Due to inherent characteristics such as high mobility and constant discon-nection, VANETs are more vulnerable to attacks. The mobility of nodes in cars and the star topology of VANETs vary rapidly. While nodes in MANET simulations move at a typical speed of 20 m/s, vehicle speeds in VANETs are even higher. This also means that V2V network interruptions are common. In particular, cars going in different directions are only able to interact with each other for a few seconds, and these gaps are regularly filled with a lack of connection in the vehicular network. Since the cars don’t see each other again, they don’t know who they are racing towards. This makes VANETs easier to target and harder to identify as suspicious cars [11] (Figure 12.3).

To ensure the dissemination of safety warnings in an open access framework, the integrity of contacts in VANETs is disrupted by attacks that involve message modification, injection, or interception. These attacks pose a significant risk to the security of VANETs, as attackers can manipulate the information in messages to further exploit vulnerabilities. VANETs face various types of threats and challenges in dealing with these dangers [12].

In VANETs, intruders aim to harm the entire network for their own gain. There are four distinct classifications of attackers in VANETs. An intruder is an attacker who is authenticated within the network, while an outsider is a non-authenticated attacker who lacks the capabilities to access the network. Active attackers are those who actively send or generate false messages, while inactive attackers do not transmit or deliver messages. Additionally, attackers can be categorized as malicious or rational. Mali-cious attackers seek to cause maximum damage without any personal benefits, whereas rational attackers aim to gain benefits. Local attackers employ a small number of vehicles to carry out attacks, while expanded attackers utilize a complete set of vehicles to target multiple networks.

It is crucial for VANETs to function securely, as a substantial understanding of threats and attacks is required. In this context, we will discuss the risks and attacks on each of the different security services found in VANETs [13].

12.4.1 Lack of Access

Accessibility of information is critical in the VANET method. In the event of accessibility issues, the reliability of VANETs can be compromised. This segment analyzes the threats and attacks on the availability of VANETs.

Denial of Service (DoS) attacks include vehicle-based denial of service attacks and external attacks on the network vehicles. Jamming of V2V communication can result in a roadblock of all available options in the networks. Distributed denial of service (DDoS) is also known as many-to-many DDoS.

12.4.2 Jamming Attack

In this scenario, the intruder utilizes a highly powerful signal that operates on the same frequency as the existing network transmission link. The main vulnerability of these devices lies in the lack of a safety signal, as the threat remains undetectable. For a jamming attack to be successful, the jammer must effectively execute an operation that simultaneously blocks the valuable signal [14].

12.4.3 Malicious Software Intrusion

The machine can be attacked through the program that supports the OBU and RSU devices. In the event of a malware attack in VANET, it is possible that the other features of the VANET framework will become inoperable.

12.4.4 Broadcast Tampering Attack

Untrustworthy vehicles may manipulate the same messages by altering the message information or creating and inserting a message within the VANETs. As a result, it is possible for hazardous incidents to remain unde-tected, which could be a contributing factor to accidents.

This is the most commonly used attack in the room, and it appears in both ad hoc and VANET environments. The most common form of attack on VANETs is carried out by registered users of VANETs. In order to determine whether or not the accused node is functioning correctly, the packets are sent to it, and it fails to accept the networking contribution. The malicious node will interfere with the routing table, and this would prevent an important message from being sent to the recipients according to the node pretending to help with the non-practical case [15].

The form of active attacks includes malicious cars, such as Smurfs and spoof packets. These Smurfs collect sensitive data, but instead of forwarding it, they dump it. Malicious vehicles abuse the MAC protocol to expand bandwidth and cost other users, known as a Greedy Behavior Attack. As a result, traffic on the transmitting channel overflows, causing a crash that disrupts the legitimate facilities of authorized customers. An attacker floods the VANET with a huge volume of spam messages, such as commercials, overloading the network by consuming more bandwidth.

Confidentiality can be assured by using credentials and distributing public keys on all exchange messages. This makes it impossible for vehicles outside of the nodes to comprehend private and classified information being communicated among the vehicles. A secure cryptographic solution protects confidentiality. Throughout this segment, we will cover the most commonly seen confidentiality risks [16].

Wired networking systems such as MANETs and VANETs often face eavesdropping attacks. The main objective of this attack is to access secure data with sensitive information. Due to this attack, information that is private to registered individuals, such as identification and location data, can be accessed by unregistered users and used to track vehicles. It is one of the classified threats that pose a challenge to traffic analysis. During this attack, the attacker analyzes the frequency of message transmission and collects as much information as possible.

12.4.5 Man-In-The-Middle Attack

In some cases, V2V contact occurs in the middle of a man-in-the-middle attack to monitor and potentially change the communications. Access and power are gained by the intruder, but contact institutions believe they should engage with each other privately.

12.4.6 Social Distraction

It is used to divert the driver’s attention from the road. When it comes to drivers, the perpetrator has an in-depth understanding of immorality and dishonest conduct. An attacker’s goal is to elicit responses from vehicles after sending messages of this nature. As a result, it affects the driving and efficiency of vehicles in the VANET scheme. A critical aspect of the VANET protocol is authentication, which is used to combat malicious nodes that may attempt to infiltrate the system. VANETs are protected from both internal and external attacks through authentication. In this section, we will explore the authentication risks in VANETs.

The first reference to the Sybil Attack is mentioned in a previous study. In this scenario, a node sends out multiple messages using false IDs. By employing certain techniques, the intruder can interfere with the vehicles’ operating systems and then utilize a V2V message mechanism to collect their responses. As a result, congestion is created on the track, leading to the need for altered routes in order to avoid obstructing other vehicles. Close to the wormhole invasion, there is a known tunneling attack. The attacker sets up a private communication link by utilizing the same infrastructure to initiate the link and then connects two different points in VANETs through a tunnel. This allows nodes that are far apart to communicate with each other. In this attack, the intruder employs deceptive techniques to generate fake GPS coordinates, tricking vehicles into believing that the coordinates are valid in another area.

12.4.7 Node Impersonation Attack

When an attacker mistakes the correct ID of the unique visitors in a VANET, they are taking part in a Node Impersonation Attack. False authentication attempts are made when being connected with a cooperative secure communication. The malicious user can make use of other users’ authentication contributions, and thus circumvent its own authentication in this attack. In this case, it is an example of free-riding action. A substantial challenge can be posed to cooperative digital signatures if this attack is effective.

A playback attack is one of the most often used attacks as well. This attack happens when legitimate data is distributed fraudulently or causes a pause to be created, resulting in an unwanted and malicious effect. In order to overcome this attack, the VANET requires a large amount of memory for a cache and a greater number of time sources to compare the messages.

Vehicle ID attacks are triggered by the use of duplicate codes and/or credentials of other cars for authentication purposes. The primary goal of this attack is to provide false information to trustworthy authorities (TAs) in order to generate doubt [17]. Attack methods include message tampering in V2V or V2I communication.

To disguise their movements, intruders use fake identities to assume the identity of another vehicle. This attack occurs when a user fails to display their own identity and instead impersonates another user to gain unauthorized access.

12.5 Compromise in Data Integrity

Ensuring data confidentiality during transfer is the only means to maintain the integrity of the data being transmitted. We will cover the various risks to data integrity in this section. A message that appears to originate from a registered node is being broadcast. In case of an emergency, identifying cars during the traffic jam may be problematic because the malicious perpetrator plans to repeat or pause the transmission by providing valid data and injecting beacons and messages received before a VANET network.

12.5.1 Error Tampering Assault

As the intruder modifies or adjusts recent message data to be sent, this attack usually occurs. An attacker can, for instance, reroute traffic in areas with congestion. This could potentially impact users’ behavior when it comes to their driving.

12.5.2 Illusion Attack

This attack gathers information from surrounding antennas and uses traffic light sensors to emit false traffic warning signals due to a hazard on the lane. Vehicle collisions and traffic congestion will result from the illusion attack. Additionally, it aims to reduce the efficiency of the VANET system by utilizing unfavorable bandwidth.

12.6 Non-Repudiation Assault

This attack occurs when the perpetrator prevents all senders and receivers from denying that messages were sent and received. The perpetrator will not communicate in case of any conflict.

12.6.1 Key Perspectives And Dimensions

Due to the significant potential in assisting motorists and passengers in maintaining their health while on the road, vehicle automation has garnered considerable attention from the ITS research community and has become one of the most prominent research areas in intelligent transportation systems (ITS).

The ITS is actively working towards providing reliable connectivity to enhance traffic management, road safety, and alleviate traffic congestion [18]. The ability to communicate V2X offers substantial benefits to the ITS, including improving traffic quality, reducing traffic fatalities, and providing real-time updates to enhance driving experiences, such as accident alerts, traffic slowdown information, traffic jam warnings, and other transportation services.

Transmission rate and latency are crucial characteristics of V2V communication. To promote V2V communication, vehicles can relay helpful information such as emergency braking, crash detection, and traffic conditions. Vehicle-to-Infrastructure (V2I) communication enables vehicles and network infrastructures to exchange valuable information.

This network connects with many other networks, including the Internet, to transmit information between vehicles and RES systems. Since they have a direct link to the networks, V2I systems need more bandwidth than V2V systems, but they are not susceptible to attack.

Now, V2X technology has recently become available. The V2X connectivity network aims to facilitate V2V communications. The 3GPP-created V2X is considered the networking technology that can perform C-V2X communications. It is capable of integrating all vehicles and, along with it, is responsible for the framework of cooperative intelligent transport systems (C-ITS) that help reduce traffic congestion and improve traffic quality.

LTE-V, long-term evolution (LTE), and C-V2X were specifications that were first announced in 2016, and they are now referred to as LTE-V, LTE, and C-V2X. The high data rate, broad coverage, and penetration rate of LTE support stable V2I communication because of this. Despite LTE’s decentralization, it faces several obstacles in V2V communication due to the lack of sufficient resources to facilitate V2V communication.

When the message is requested to be sent to a single vehicle or to a group of vehicles, use this notification. As an example, an alert will be sent to the drivers still on their route to stop traffic delays and improve traffic safety if an accident or crash occurs. The method for sending the alert messages to just the destination is a new routing algorithm, which is needed to manage this problem [19].

12.6.2 Vehicle-To-Vehicle (V2V) Group Communication

Only vehicles that share common features can participate in this communication. This includes vehicles of the same brand and their respective locations at a given time. Vehicle Beaconing: Vehicles in the vicinity receive periodic signals, and RSUs are also notified. These messages contain information about the speed, direction, and speed of the receiving vehicle.

If there is a likelihood of collision or accident, warnings for vehicular traffic are transmitted from the connectivity via RSUs to all vehicles within the vicinity. This is especially important when the roadway is curving, an intersection is encountered, or the road is narrow.

12.7 Conclusion And Future Scope

One of the main elements of wireless architecture is VANET, in which vehicular devices are interconnected with advanced protocols and algo-rithms. In some cases, Intelligent Transportation Networks are used with VANETs for Intelligent Communications on the road. An understanding is given to the notion that they have developed into something more expan-sive: “the Internet of Vehicles (IoT).” This integration predicts that these larger concepts will eventually develop into a concept with the name “the Internet of autonomous vehicles.” Wireless networking technologies, such as Wi-Fi, can be used as the basis for VANETs or Wi-Fi, medium-range radio technologies like AMI broadcasting, and long-range radio technologies like SAT-radio (either standard Wi-Fi or ZigBee). Cellular networks or LTE are also useful for VANETs. Visible light contact (VLC) is the latest in this networking technology (Infrared transmission and reception). The rapid use of vehicular ad hoc networks (VANETs) is bringing the internet of things (IoT) to the forefront. The manuscript presents the assorted perspectives of VANET with real-world assaults, which should be removed so that the cumulative environment will be safe and secure from different assaults. The future scope of the work is integrated with the usage of blockchain-based technologies and protocols with a security-based environment. Nowadays, blockchain technologies are quite prominent for a security-aware environment; the work can be associated with advanced implementations of blockchain.
